Quantum Computing's First Real-world Payoff May Come In Energy Solutions (cnbc.com) 1
			
		 	
				While quantum computing's biggest breakthroughs "are still likely to be years, if not decades, away," CNBC reports the technology is already being tested in energy applications, "from keeping power grids stable to finding better materials for solar cells and hydrogen production."
Quantinuum, which was formed by the merger of Cambridge Quantum and Honeywell Quantum Solutions and listed on Nasdaq in June, is using the technology to model materials for hydrogen fuel cells for transportation. It is also working with partners on materials for carbon capture.  Another U.K. player, ORCA Computing, a spinout from the University of Oxford, has partnered with BP to better understand how a molecule's physical, chemical and biological properties are governed. That could help explain how biofuels burn and how to make them burn more efficiently...  For [quantum software company] Phasecraft, a priority is grid stability and optimization. It has partnered with the National Energy System Operator, the independent system planner that balances energy supply and demand in England, Scotland and Wales, on grid optimization tasks, including managing power exchanges and isolating network sections during emergencies. The company says its software can complete these tasks up to 1,000 times faster than conventional computing... 
[Phasecraft] is also working with U.K. solar cell maker Oxford PV on the development of new materials for photovoltaic cells capable of operating more efficiently than existing ones.  Separately, it has teamed up with London-listed specialty materials developer Johnson Matthey, Harvard University and Boston-based quantum hardware specialist QuEra, in work overseen by the U.S. government's Advanced Research Projects Agency-Energy (ARPA-E), to discover novel catalysts that would reduce reliance on scarce metals like iridium — which is currently critical in the production of hydrogen. Asked why Phasecraft is focusing on energy rather than sectors that might seem more lucrative, [cofounder/CEO Ashley] Montanaro said the returns were there too.  "When you get this right, the commercial returns are real: more efficient energy networks mean lower operating costs and more value from existing infrastructure," he said.

The Engineering Manager's role is under attack (again)
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-22
07:01:00 UTC
16:01:00 KST

The Engineering Manager's role is under attack (again)
And nobody learns from past mistakes
Every time I read about another company laying off managers and ‘running flatter’, I think about how absurd it is. I can’t understand why senior leaders don’t see the harm they inflict on their own companies.
It’s also quite personal, of course.
So I decided to speak with someone who’s been through more of these cycles than I have. I’ve been enjoying Suzan’s newsletter for years - she’s an organizational development consultant who works with tech leaders. After reading her article about being laid off 6(!) times before becoming a COO, I knew we had to talk.
Today we’ll cover:
- Why the EM role specifically is under attack
- Repeating history - Google and Microsoft already ran this experiment
- How flattening impacts the company
- What EMs can do about it
Mic to Suzan!
Why the EM role specifically is under attack
There is this common idea that with AI, we can run much leaner and that agents can replace much of the human work.
The first attack was on software engineers: for a short while, leaders claimed that the profession was dead. That turned around pretty quickly, with companies understanding they still need experienced engineers to decide what to build, review what LLMs produce, and maintain the systems afterward. Engineers now make up 55% of Big Tech hiring, up from 46% in 2019.
But with managers, it’s harder to justify, and we are repeating the flattening cycle that we went through 10 years ago. Some of the bigger tech companies started by laying off middle managers, and the smaller companies started to think, “Oh, I should consider that too” (whether their own conditions are the same or not).
And when leaders look at the first-line Engineering Manager’s role, they think: “Well, they’re close to the work. Maybe they should just be doing the work? Maybe we don’t need all of them? What value do they actually provide?”
I hear from managers all around me that the number of people reporting to them keeps increasing. Meta is saying a 1-to-50 ratio is their goal, and 15-20 engineers reporting to a manager is becoming very common.
Combine that with the expectation that EMs also ship code, and you quickly start to experience the EM’s role erosion cycle:
- Less capacity. You have less capacity to actually manage people.
- Less managing. So you do less of the work only you can do. You do fewer 1:1s, postpone career conversations, and don’t deal with team conflicts or processes.
- Less value. Which means you provide less value to the engineers reporting to you, and they get used to managing without you.
- Less needed. And the less value you provide as a manager, the less ‘needed’ the leader thinks you are. So they increase the engineer-to-manager ratio again.
And this cycle isn’t new. Tech companies get into this delusion that the ‘people stuff’ doesn’t really matter, and then they wake up and quietly start adding back layers:
We are repeating history
This trend of getting rid of managers has been tried before.
In 2002, when Google had ~300 engineers, Larry Page and Sergey Brin tried to completely remove all managers. It failed after just a couple of months:
“They relented when too many people went directly to Page with questions about expense reports, interpersonal conflicts, and other nitty-gritty issues. And as the company grew, the founders soon realized that managers contributed in many other, important ways - for instance, by communicating strategy, helping employees prioritize projects, facilitating collaboration, supporting career development, and ensuring that processes and systems aligned with company goals.”
Six years later (with 20k+ employees), Google decided to test again if managers are important. They ran Project Oxygen, which showed that managers did matter (surprise), with good managers strongly correlating with team performance and engineer satisfaction.
And now, Google is flattening again - cutting about 10% of manager, director, and VP roles.
It’s a similar story at Microsoft:
- 2014 - Satya Nadella announced a ‘major restructuring’: fewer management layers and wider spans of control.
- 2025 - 11 years later, Microsoft flattened again to ‘increase span of control’.
Layoffs and flattening are most often sudden and make the news headlines, but the recovery is less exciting. No company suddenly hires hundreds of managers, they slowly rebuild those layers over time.
But why? If companies consistently believe that managers are not needed, how come they keep hiring managers again and again?
How flattening impacts the company
You can actually run ‘flat’ for a while. It takes a few months or years before companies experience the impact and the pendulum begins to swing back.
What happens is that companies start to accumulate organizational debt - similar to tech debt, a gradual buildup of dysfunction that undermines how good work gets done.
Relational debt is the type of org debt that accumulates the fastest after a flattening. It’s strained, neglected, or transactional relationships where trust and psychological safety erode. When you expand an EM’s role to both be more technical and have more reports, something has to give - you’re chipping away at the people part of the company, and that has consequences.
There’s more negative behavior turning into toxic behavior, more conflicts, and less collaboration.
And that’s even worse with AI:
One of the things managers repeatedly ask me about in workshops is: “How do I keep the team together when they prefer to talk to AI rather than talk to each other?”
So in addition to reduced human contact between managers and employees, engineers are also less in contact with each other.
And once you remove that human connection, you start having more toxic behavior and conflict. And when that conflict occurs, leaders treat it as ‘one bad apple’. The response is often to just get rid of ‘the problematic person’. Well, that person is operating inside an environment, and that poor environment does more damage to your company goals than shipping speed ever makes up for.
Every company has its own problems. When you rip out layers of management, you basically deprioritize people - which will only magnify your existing problems and will make it much harder to deal with the massive change currently happening in the industry.
What EMs can do about it
Senior leaders are getting messages from the market right now. “You can go faster and leaner, you don’t need all those people, use AI and reach your goals faster.”
That makes them anxious, thinking: “Everyone else is doing it, why can’t we?”
The best thing an EM can do is provide grounded reality. The more information we give them, the better decisions they make, and the closer we’ll get to breaking the collective delusion a lot of us are under right now.
Here’s what I suggest to start from:
1. Write it down. Spend time capturing what the real problems are. Get it out of your head so that you can really see them.
It’ll probably not be one or two big problems, but 10-15 smaller ones. Some conflicts between engineers and the PM, engineers not working well together, etc. It’s much easier to come to your manager with a big problem. With 10 small ones, you feel like your already overloaded manager just wouldn’t care.
The next step is to help them care:
2. Attach them to something bigger.
When you’re looking at the 10 little problems, are they all different shapes? Or can you find patterns? If not, how do you convey something that looks small in a way that attaches it to something bigger?
For example, a disagreement between an engineer and the PM. No director wants to hear about that. But the PM ended up not listening to the engineer, so the team built what was asked for and threw most of it away two weeks later. Add to that two engineers who don’t work well together and shipped overlapping implementations of the same thing a week later.
How can you frame that in a way they’ll care about?
Translating the challenges you’re seeing on your teams as obstacles to business goals is always a good move. Think about what your senior leaders care about most. Start with questions like:
- What are the company's top priorities?
- What does your Director or VP talk about most?
- What keeps them up at night?
Then map your list of problems to what your leaders care about. Pick the one that you think is most critical or endangering a current project.
Final words
Thanks Suzan for a great conversation and guest article!
To be clear, I'm not saying more management layers are always better, or that there's no benefit in simplifying the chain of command. But there's a big difference between cutting managers because managers don't matter (as many flattening companies claim), and trying to fix your own org structure.
I believe that your first- and second-line engineering managers are often the ones holding the org together. I don’t think it’s a coincidence that just a couple of months after Meta laid off 700 engineering managers, it’s now facing a serious resignation wave.
What I enjoyed reading this week
- The Same Side of the Table. How to deal with meetings that include your engineers (and people from outside the team).
- Lenses: Forgetting, Contributing, and "Not-doing”. Loved all the 3 quotes here.
- Why Ramp built its own in-house coding agent, Inspect. An AMAZING deep dive into engineering at Ramp. Many takeaways for me there.
Get the next one in your inbox
One email a week for engineering managers. No spam, unsubscribe anytime.

Notes on the discrete Fourier transform (DFT)
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-22
06:52:25 UTC
15:52:25 KST

In the previous post on this topic, we’ve seen how the Discrete-Time Fourier Series (DTFS) and transform (DTFT) are used to analyze infinite-duration periodic and non-periodic discrete signals. Now, it’s time to turn to realistic digital signal processing where signals are not only discrete, but also finite in duration.
Analyzing signal samples
Suppose we have samples of a signal - it’s a list of numbers:
We want to analyze the frequencies present in this signal; in other words, to find the coefficients in a series of complex exponentials that reconstructs the signal. One option is to assume that is an infinite domain signal with zero values outside our samples, and then compute its DTFT:
However, this is a continuous function of frequency , so it’s difficult compute on an actual computer; we cannot evaluate and store it for all , so we have to choose which to compute it for. We'll soon see how this choice is made.
The Discrete Fourier Transform (DFT)
But there's an alternative way to look at the problem. We only have samples of the signal , so we can assume they represent a single period of a periodic signal. And in that case, we can use DTFS; recall that the coefficients of the series are:
As we’ve seen in the post on the DTFS, this series can be used to reconstruct a discrete, periodic signal precisely. Since our signal is finite and we assume it’s just one period of a periodic signal, it means we can also reconstruct precisely using a DTFS.
This is the main insight behind the Discrete Fourier Transform (DFT). First, by convention [1] we define:
And then:
This is called the Discrete Fourier Transform of . The term transform may be a bit puzzling here - after all, we’re just using the series coefficients - so isn’t it more proper to just say series? Perhaps, though in mathematics a transform has a pretty general meaning of "a mapping that takes a mathematical object and produces another object"; in that sense, "transform" is proper here because we take an object - and produce another object - .
Moreover, DFT and DTFT are intimately related. Recall how we said earlier that the DTFT is tricky to compute because is a continuous variable? Well, the DFT gives us a way to sample that variable into the discrete domain:
So the DFT can be seen as exactly equally spaced samples of the DTFT.
Computing the DFT
Since the goal of this post is specifically to demonstrate computable methods of applying Fourier analysis to discrete digital signals, here’s the Python code to calculate the DFT of a signal:
def dft(x):
    """Return X[k] = sum_n x[n] exp(-2 pi i k n / N)."""
    N = len(x)
    n = np.arange(N)
    X = np.empty(N, dtype=complex)
    for k in range(N):
        weights = np.exp(-2j * np.pi * k * n / N)
        X[k] = np.sum(x * weights)
    return X
It shouldn’t be hard to see the correspondence between this code and the mathematical formula for . Python’s Numpy code is vectorized by default, so the weights array is computed in whole for every k (because n is an array of indices ).
DFT and the complex roots of unity
In my earlier post on the complex roots of unity, we’ve seen how powers of the roots of unity form an orthogonal basis for . Now let’s show the beautiful theoretical connection between this fact and the DFT.
Recall that the -th roots of unity can be written as:
Since all are just powers of , we can write them as:
The conjugates of the roots of unity are roots of unity themselves:
With being the same as , so when we enumerate all the roots of unity, we’re also enumerating their conjugates, just in a reversed order.
With that in mind, let’s denote [2] , and examine the sequence:
The vectors formed by (element-wise) raising to integer powers from to can be collected into a matrix:
In the roots of unity post, we’ve proven that the rows of this matrix form an orthogonal basis over . In other words, any vector in can be represented as a linear combination of the rows of .
I hope you can see where this is going! Let’s consider the signal as a column vector in . The DFT of can be computed by multiplying by the matrix :
Take a minute to convince yourself this is true, by working through the multiplication for a specific row , e.g. is computed via row-column multiplication the third row (counting from 0) of and .
Our is called the Fourier Matrix or the DFT Matrix. Previously we’ve seen Python code that directly computes the DFT; here’s a version that does this via the matrix multiplication approach:
def dft_matrix(N):
    """Construct F[k, n] = W_N**(k*n), where W_N = exp(-2 pi i / N)."""
    # W_N is a primitive N-th root of unity: W_N**N = 1.
    # Its powers give all N roots of z**N - 1 = 0.
    root = np.exp(-2j * np.pi / N)
    indices = np.arange(N)
    # Rows correspond to frequency bins k; columns to sample indices n.
    exponents = np.outer(indices, indices)
    return root**exponents
def dft_via_matrix(x):
    """Compute the DFT as F @ x with the Fourier matrix"""
    return dft_matrix(len(x)) @ x
More than the computational convenience though, there’s theoretical elegance here. Back when we were discussing the continuous Fourier series, we’ve seen how the trigonometric functions (or - equivalently - complex exponentials) form an orthogonal basis for square integrable functions.
Here, we see a similar linear algebra connection in discrete time - the vectors formed from powers of complex roots of unity form an orthogonal basis in and can thus be used to represent any sequence of complex values.
Which brings us to the next topic: reconstructing a signal from its DFT representation.
Inverse DFT: reconstructing the signal
There are two equivalent approaches to find the inverse DFT - the formula we can use to reconstruct given . The first approach is going back to DTFS, where the reconstruction formula is:
Recall that our convention is . Therefore:
This is the Inverse Discrete Fourier Transform formula.
Let’s examine the other approach now, because it uses our linear algebra interpretation in an enlightening way.
Recall the Fourier matrix , and consider - the same matrix where every element is the conjugate of the corresponding element of . If:
Then:
What happens if we multiply by ?
We have seen in the roots of unity post that this amounts to:
In other words:
Where is the identity matrix. Since is a square matrix, any matrix that multiplies with it to give the identity is its inverse:
Recall that in vector form:
Therefore:
With our definition of :
And in sum form:
Which is the same reconstruction formula we obtained from the DTFS. In mathematics, it’s always a good sign when we obtain the same result using different methods!
The linear algebra interpretation here is clear: the vectors formed by the powers of roots of unity form an orthogonal basis over . Therefore, every vector can be represented as a linear combination of these vectors. Moreover, this transformation is invertible, and the original vector can be reconstructed by left-multiplying its DFT vector by .
Example
It’s time for a fully worked example; the entire Python code for this example is available on GitHub; read through its main function, which is well commented.
Recall the sampled triangular signal we’ve used in the previous post; here, let’s use a single cycle:
In code, this is just an array of numbers:
x = np.array([0, 1, 2, 3, 2, 1, 0, -1, -2, -3, -2, -1]) / 3
Calculating the DFT of this signal using the code shown earlier produces the following values; remember that consists of complex numbers, so we represent them as a magnitude-phase pair. Phase is only shown for nonzero coefficients, because the phase of zero is undefined [3]:
Obviously, we’d get exactly the same numbers by using the matrix multiplication method of computing the DFT. And to reconstruct the signal, we multiply by the computed IDFT matrix to get the original signal back. The reconstruction matches the original signal precisely, with any differences being attributable to floating point round-off.
Efficiently computing the DFT
The sample code contains another method of computing the DFT of this signal - by calling NumPy’s fft.fft function. This refers to the Fast Fourier Transform, which is considered to be one of the most important algorithms of the 20th century. By exploiting the structure of the Fourier matrix , FFT computes the product in operations, compared with for direct matrix-vector multiplication. I plan to cover how this works in a future post.
Appendix: spectral leakage
Looking carefully at the signal used in the Example section above, we see that it’s actually one full cycle out of a sampled triangle wave. This is convenient, because repeating the block reproduces the original triangle wave. But what happens when the samples cover only part of the cycle?
Take for example just the first 10 samples of the triangle wave:
If we naively extend this signal periodically, things won’t line up (there will be a jump at the end of every cycle). We can still calculate its DFT, of course; these are the coefficients we’ll get:
While the coefficients still come in conjugate pairs (because the samples are real), the original triangle’s frequencies no longer coincide with the DFT’s frequency bins, so their contributions spread across multiple bins. This is called spectral leakage.
By applying the IDFT we can still reconstruct the original 10-sample signal perfectly! So if all we need is reconstruction, spectral leakage isn’t an issue; but if we’re doing frequency analysis, it skews the situation somewhat, adding frequencies that didn’t really exist in the underlying triangle wave - just to account for the discontinuity. In real-world signal processing, such issues are usually addressed by applying special window functions to the signal after sampling - these help alleviate leakage by tapering the signal close to zero at the edges.

Show HN: The Riemann Proof Handbook
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-22
05:46:30 UTC
14:46:30 KST

A book, in PDF and EPUB, that writes the Lean proof of the Riemann Hypothesis in tamnd/riemann-lean as mathematics: definitions, theorems, lemmas and their proofs, in words.
The claim is not checked. Nobody has built the Lean project in full yet, and no person has checked the written statements against the Lean code. This book explains the Lean code. It is not a separate check of the mathematics. See What is checked.
The books are in the releases.
Each declaration of the proof is inside the dominated subtree of exactly one other declaration (its immediate dominator, in the graph of uses from riemannHypothesis). The ledger gives an entry to each declaration whose dominated subtree is large, in tiers: 1,000 or more declarations (591 entries), 300 or more (1,927), 100 or more (5,764). It assigns each entry to a volume by its branch of the proof or by its Lean folder, and to a chapter by its Lean folder. In a chapter, the entries come from the top of the proof down.
python -m rhb ledger --records /path/to/records --min 1000 --write
ledger/coverage.md has the numbers. prompts/entry.md is the brief for the writers of entries.
You need Python 3.11 or later, pandoc 3.8 or later and Tectonic. Put tamnd/riemann and tamnd/riemann-lean next to this repository.
pip install .
python -m rhb survey
python -m rhb check
python -m rhb build --volume 0,1,2 --formats pdf,epub
The books go to build/. The pipeline is that of The Handbook of Algorithms: Markdown, then pandoc with filters/rhb.lua, then LaTeX with tex/handbook.sty, then Tectonic. The EPUB is EPUB 3 with MathML.
The text is licensed under CC BY 4.0, as the text of tamnd/riemann. The builder is licensed under the MIT licence.
Please report errors at issues.

AI Supply Chain Security: What Canary Tokens Found
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-22
05:11:43 UTC
14:11:43 KST

HD Moore, Chief Research Officer, will be regularly sharing work from the research team he leads at Dragos. Topics will include xOT, IT, LLMs, PQC, and more. Their findings will sharpen defenders’ understanding of exposures and vulnerabilities emerging across the threat landscape.
We sent nearly two million AI prompts containing unique, trackable secrets to AI providers, then watched where those secrets surfaced. What we found revealed security breaches, prompt theft, and widespread failures to protect customer data.
Over a five-month period from July through November 2026, we submitted approximately two million prompts containing unique URLs and canary token credentials to widely used AI services and monitored what happened after inference. Our investigation uncovered three significant findings:
- Evidence of provider compromises. We observed activity indicating that data from at least three AI providers was exposed. Our seeded test prompts were used in attempts to access our controlled test systems and canary AWS accounts.
- Unauthorized access to customer prompts by inference marketplace providers. Multiple resellers operating through an AI inference marketplace were able to view and retain customer prompts using standard logging capabilities, without exploiting a vulnerability or bypassing platform controls.
- Failures in privacy and data-handling practices. We observed multiple cases in which providers appeared not to honor customer privacy settings or published policies. Examples included prompts submitted to U.S.-based providers being transmitted to Chinese AI organizations, unique URLs being accessed months after a single inference request, and one seeded link ultimately appearing in an internal company chat in China.
We wanted to understand what happens to inference data after it leaves the customer’s environment.
To answer that question, we created millions of unique canaries, including URLs and credentials that would never be used by legitimate systems. Any attempt to access those assets would indicate that someone, somewhere in the AI ecosystem had seen the original prompt.
The results exposed a fundamental disconnect between how AI services are marketed and how inference data is handled in practice.
Most folks picture AI inference as a question and a response. Text goes in, GPUs juggle some bits, and text comes back out. What complicates this is how the data is actually treated.
AI providers commonly store prompts and responses for operational, training, safety, abuse-monitoring, or business purposes. Depending on the provider, that data may be retained for days, months, or indefinitely. It may also traverse multiple organizations, including model developers, cloud operators, inference platforms, and third-party resellers.
The prompts and replies – the inference data itself – are often more valuable than the GPU resources used to process them. They frequently contain confidential source code, internal communications, strategic plans, customer information, credentials, and other sensitive business data.
AI routers work by offering a single API endpoint that forwards your request to a pool of available models, sometimes at different hosting providers, with unified billing. Routers are great at cost reduction and providing flexibility without managing multiple accounts and credentials. The path to the inference server often passes through more than one router before reaching another provider, and from there the specific server hosting the target model. There is risk in this process – every hop can see the entire prompt and response.
Some routers are open marketplaces where the seller with the lowest price gets the request. It’s common for sellers to offer AI inference at deep discounts, and they can do this by pooling requests through unmetered consumer-plan subscriptions or by finding other low-cost paths to the specific model (startup credits, etc.). In the shadier marketplaces, it’s also common to see inference that is billed to stolen AWS credentials or consumer API keys. If a seller wants to siphon customer request data, they can list popular models at the lowest price, route the traffic, pay the difference, and then collect the data from the platform logs.
These providers offer privacy controls – customers can set privacy flags at the account level, for a specific API key, or in each request. The strictest setting is known as Zero Data Retention (ZDR), which specifies that the prompt and response are not stored. The routers should only forward these requests to providers that offer the same controls. The reality is quite different – providers often ignore these settings, either intentionally or due to bugs in their processing flow, and prompts not covered by ZDR are fair game for resale into a massive ecosystem of buyers and sellers.
The most serious finding was also the most direct. We found attackers actively stealing data from three different hosting providers. Although our prompts were bait, they were processed along with real customer requests, and those were almost certainly copied too. The earliest incident dates back to the middle of July, and none of the three providers knew about their breach until we notified them.
The largest breach involved a couple hundred of our AWS keys. Each key was sent in exactly one request to one provider, all within a few short windows in late October, and every request we sent during those windows was stolen. Some of the stolen keys came from requests that the provider rejected before a model ever ran – this tells us that the attacker was copying requests at the provider’s front door, and not somewhere deeper in the inference stack. In the other two breaches, some of the stolen requests were marked as ZDR. A privacy flag doesn’t help when the attacker is already inside.
Once the attacker had our credentials, they followed a predictable path. First, they tested each key to see if it was live, in some cases within a few hours of our request. Next, they went looking for data, listing S3 buckets and searching Secrets Manager and Parameter Store. Finally, they tried enabling hosted models in AWS Bedrock, creating Bedrock API keys, and adding their own IAM users to keep access. In total, our bait keys were used for tens of thousands of AWS API calls.
Figure 1. One stolen AWS key. We seeded it on October 21, it was first used the next day, and it was still in use three weeks later. Names are pseudonyms and addresses are partly masked.
One adversary appears to have compromised two of these providers at the same time. Secrets that we sent to those two providers were used together, almost immediately, from a single rented server. The attacker’s tooling changed over the three weeks that we watched it – it started with a script pretending to be Chrome on Windows, moved to Boto3 running inside OrbStack, and later switched to the AWS CLI on Kali Linux.
On November 1, the same attacker added ARTEX, an LLM-driven penetration testing framework. In our case, ARTEX did very little – it made a handful of calls to confirm that keys the attacker already held were still live. Two of the user agents it sent, artex-agent and artex-judge-r121, don’t appear in any public indicator list that we could find, and are worth adding to your detections.
Figure 2. Tools that were used against our planted secrets, including the AI pentest agent (first row) and the Kali environment (aws-cli row).
The good news for defenders is that these tools tend to announce themselves – both Kali and ARTEX showed up in the user agent that AWS records in CloudTrail. The bad news is that this self-identification was only present in a small fraction of traffic.
The second finding didn’t require an exploit at all. The marketplace sellers that we caught only had to list a model at the lowest price, enable request logging, and wait. What stood out is how personal it was – these were individuals, working from what looked like home internet connections and personal computers, trying to break into our canary servers one login at a time.
In one case, we sent two prompts through a single seller’s offer. About a day and a half later, someone on a home broadband connection opened both secret URLs in a desktop browser and started guessing usernames at the login page. Prompts for the same model that went through a different seller were never touched, which tells us that the leak follows the seller and not the marketplace as a whole.
In another case, an attacker working from a Mac on a home broadband line in France took the passwords from two of our prompts and tried to log in as root over SSH, typing each attempt by hand. The same Mac later tested several of our AWS keys in a single session.
Figure 3. One of the root SSH attempts. The password from our prompt was used four times in two minutes, and the route trace shows the two routers that the prompt passed through.
Most of those keys had been sent through the marketplace, but one had gone to a completely different router. It turned out that this router was quietly forwarding some of its traffic to the marketplace – another example of an undisclosed hop.
The third finding is different from the first two, as nobody used a credential. Instead, the secret URLs in our prompts were scraped, repeatedly, and the git repositories they referenced were targeted for cloning – all by companies that were never supposed to see the prompt in the first place, and often in direct conflict with the privacy settings on the request.
The clearest example involves a Chinese AI lab. This lab’s systems fetched the secret URLs from more than half of the prompts that we sent to its models between July and October, including roughly two-thirds of the requests marked as “no data collection” or Zero Data Retention. The scraper typically showed up two or three days after the request.
This wasn’t limited to requests sent to the lab directly. Several U.S. hosting providers resell this lab’s models, and prompts sent to those providers triggered the exact same scraper – the prompts were flowing back to the lab. A customer who picked a U.S. provider specifically to keep their data in the U.S. didn’t get what they paid for. Later on, a git client on a different cloud started trying to clone the private repositories referenced in our prompts, but only for prompts that used this lab’s models.
The large U.S. providers have their own issues. One continued to crawl a secret URL more than two months after we used it in a single inference call to their search endpoint. Another runs a scraper that comes back for our URLs days after each request, regardless of which router we used to reach it.
Figure 4. One day of incidents, grouped by source network. The same scraper networks show up against prompts that we sent through several different routers.
The strangest incident involved a company chat. Twenty-four days after we sent a prompt to one Chinese provider, the secret link from that prompt was posted into a Feishu chat channel (Feishu is known as Lark outside of China). Within two minutes, the link was opened from three desktops and three phones in Beijing and Shanghai. Our disclosure to this provider only included the first eight characters of that link, so whoever shared it was reading from a stored copy of the full prompt, more than three weeks after the request was sent to a U.S.-based provider with the ZDR flag.
Figure 5. The secret link shared in a chat and then opened in the mobile app. The git client is the earlier clone attempt.
Zero Data Retention is supposed to mean something. In practice, these prompts were retained, shared with other companies, and read by people.
A canary token is a secret that only exists in one place – if anyone ever uses it, you know that place has been exposed. We started in July by seeding prompts with Thinkst Canarytokens, and every request carried an AWS key, a DNS name, and a URL that had never been seen anywhere else. Tokens started firing within days, and the tokens from July are still firing today.
The proof-of-concept worked so well that it made sense to build a dedicated service just for seeding LLM prompts. This service generates a unique seed, and uses it to derive unique hostnames, URLs, and credentials for every prompt and then runs the services that those credentials point to – DNS, web, git, SSH, databases, mail, and a long tail of older protocols. The AWS keys are real keys tied to empty accounts with full logging. Every request that touches a token is tied back to the exact prompt, model, router, and privacy setting that carried it. Since each unique component can be tied back to its parent seed, we’re able to get precise timing and sequencing; we know what DNS server they use, how long it took to reply, and every piece of the inbound connections (TCP, TLS SNI, HTTP Host headers, URLs, credentials etc.).
Figure 6. The console overview, showing incidents and seeded prompts per day, split by the router that handled the prompt.
Canaries tell us that a prompt leaked, but not where in the chain, since a router typically reports a single upstream provider, and that provider is often just another router. To figure out who was really handling our requests, we built something like traceroute for AI inference. The first part is simple – we include an image URL in the request that points back to our servers, and every system that fetches it gives away an IP address, an HTTP client, and a timestamp.
The second part takes advantage of how these services are stacked. We send small, slightly broken requests, and since each router tends to wrap the error from the hop behind it in its own format, a single response can contain three or four nested “envelopes.” Matching each layer against a signature database gives us the list of hops, the software at each one, the network they use, and often the company operating it.
So far, we have traced about two thousand routes. Several hundred of those included a system that we couldn’t identify, and a handful sent our image to a company that wasn’t on the advertised path at all. The traces also turned up middle layers that the route never mentions – one marketplace seller quietly adds about a thousand hidden tokens to every prompt, and another fetches images through a shared consumer subscription account.
Figure 7. An example of LLM route tracing, showing a path from SurplusIntelligence to Anthropic. The OpenRouter hop was discovered by tracing and was not disclosed in the marketplace
Every incident is stored with its evidence – the original prompt, the route it took, every connection that followed, and our notes. The first incident was mid-July; the provider first thought this was a misconfiguration of internal tooling, then a problem with partner data processing, until the investigation uncovered evidence that the attacker had compromised their internal credentials too. This provider has still not publicly disclosed the incident and the stolen data continued to lead to attempted logins as of late October.
For the second batch of intrusions in late October, we reached out as soon as we confirmed that the data provided evidence of third-party access, and one of these providers has already published a preliminary security notice.
For the less severe issues like data sharing (but not credential abuse), we started notifying providers in early November. Each report included the list of stolen requests along with unique strings that they could search for in their own logs. We are now working with about a dozen providers and wouldn’t be surprised to see this expand.
Reactions have been mixed. The router that was forwarding traffic to the marketplace told us that they stopped, and several providers have responded and started their own investigations. On the other hand, stolen keys and secret URLs continue to be accessed, even months after the initial incident.
None of this means that you should stop using AI services. It does mean that where your prompts go is a decision, and if you don’t make it, the lowest price will make it for you. Keep in mind that a privacy flag only binds the first hop, and that AI agents will happily send whatever they can read, including credentials.
- Host it yourself, if you can afford it. This is the only option where nobody else gets to read your prompts, but the hardware isn’t cheap. A Mac Studio with 512 GB of memory starts at $22,299, NVIDIA’s DGX Spark now costs $6,950, a DGX Station starts at about $105,000, and an eight-GPU B300 server lists for $570,000 or more. For most teams, this only makes sense for the most sensitive work.
- Go directly to a first-tier provider. One company, one contract, and one privacy policy to read. This can still leak through data sharing agreements or a breach at the provider, but it is the smallest exposure you can buy.
- Use routers with care. Pick the upstream providers yourself instead of accepting the default pool, and verify that your privacy settings actually change where your requests go. Stay away from reseller marketplaces – some of that capacity comes from stolen keys, and a few of these services only accept cryptocurrency for a reason.
- Seed your own canaries. Add a fake credential to the context that your agents and pipelines already send and treat any use of it as an incident. A free Canarytoken from Thinkst is enough to get started today.
- Enable secret filtering. Some providers offer settings that will automatically scrub known credential formats from prompt data. This can’t hurt, but it’s not a panacea – there is plenty of sensitive data in prompts that isn’t shaped like an API key.
This research effort felt a lot like what we do at Dragos with runZero; using small bits of data to provide a comprehensive view of networks from multiple perspectives. Would you like that kind of visibility into your own environment?
PS. If you found this interesting, check out Lauren Proehl’s post, I leaked credentials on purpose for four months. Here’s who took them, and what they did next.

====================================================================================================

AI Supply Chain Security: What Canary Tokens Found
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-22
05:11:43 UTC
14:11:43 KST

HD Moore, Chief Research Officer, will be regularly sharing work from the research team he leads at Dragos. Topics will include xOT, IT, LLMs, PQC, and more. Their findings will sharpen defenders’ understanding of exposures and vulnerabilities emerging across the threat landscape.
We sent nearly two million AI prompts containing unique, trackable secrets to AI providers, then watched where those secrets surfaced. What we found revealed security breaches, prompt theft, and widespread failures to protect customer data.
Over a five-month period from July through November 2026, we submitted approximately two million prompts containing unique URLs and canary token credentials to widely used AI services and monitored what happened after inference. Our investigation uncovered three significant findings:
- Evidence of provider compromises. We observed activity indicating that data from at least three AI providers was exposed. Our seeded test prompts were used in attempts to access our controlled test systems and canary AWS accounts.
- Unauthorized access to customer prompts by inference marketplace providers. Multiple resellers operating through an AI inference marketplace were able to view and retain customer prompts using standard logging capabilities, without exploiting a vulnerability or bypassing platform controls.
- Failures in privacy and data-handling practices. We observed multiple cases in which providers appeared not to honor customer privacy settings or published policies. Examples included prompts submitted to U.S.-based providers being transmitted to Chinese AI organizations, unique URLs being accessed months after a single inference request, and one seeded link ultimately appearing in an internal company chat in China.
We wanted to understand what happens to inference data after it leaves the customer’s environment.
To answer that question, we created millions of unique canaries, including URLs and credentials that would never be used by legitimate systems. Any attempt to access those assets would indicate that someone, somewhere in the AI ecosystem had seen the original prompt.
The results exposed a fundamental disconnect between how AI services are marketed and how inference data is handled in practice.
Most folks picture AI inference as a question and a response. Text goes in, GPUs juggle some bits, and text comes back out. What complicates this is how the data is actually treated.
AI providers commonly store prompts and responses for operational, training, safety, abuse-monitoring, or business purposes. Depending on the provider, that data may be retained for days, months, or indefinitely. It may also traverse multiple organizations, including model developers, cloud operators, inference platforms, and third-party resellers.
The prompts and replies – the inference data itself – are often more valuable than the GPU resources used to process them. They frequently contain confidential source code, internal communications, strategic plans, customer information, credentials, and other sensitive business data.
AI routers work by offering a single API endpoint that forwards your request to a pool of available models, sometimes at different hosting providers, with unified billing. Routers are great at cost reduction and providing flexibility without managing multiple accounts and credentials. The path to the inference server often passes through more than one router before reaching another provider, and from there the specific server hosting the target model. There is risk in this process – every hop can see the entire prompt and response.
Some routers are open marketplaces where the seller with the lowest price gets the request. It’s common for sellers to offer AI inference at deep discounts, and they can do this by pooling requests through unmetered consumer-plan subscriptions or by finding other low-cost paths to the specific model (startup credits, etc.). In the shadier marketplaces, it’s also common to see inference that is billed to stolen AWS credentials or consumer API keys. If a seller wants to siphon customer request data, they can list popular models at the lowest price, route the traffic, pay the difference, and then collect the data from the platform logs.
These providers offer privacy controls – customers can set privacy flags at the account level, for a specific API key, or in each request. The strictest setting is known as Zero Data Retention (ZDR), which specifies that the prompt and response are not stored. The routers should only forward these requests to providers that offer the same controls. The reality is quite different – providers often ignore these settings, either intentionally or due to bugs in their processing flow, and prompts not covered by ZDR are fair game for resale into a massive ecosystem of buyers and sellers.
The most serious finding was also the most direct. We found attackers actively stealing data from three different hosting providers. Although our prompts were bait, they were processed along with real customer requests, and those were almost certainly copied too. The earliest incident dates back to the middle of July, and none of the three providers knew about their breach until we notified them.
The largest breach involved a couple hundred of our AWS keys. Each key was sent in exactly one request to one provider, all within a few short windows in late October, and every request we sent during those windows was stolen. Some of the stolen keys came from requests that the provider rejected before a model ever ran – this tells us that the attacker was copying requests at the provider’s front door, and not somewhere deeper in the inference stack. In the other two breaches, some of the stolen requests were marked as ZDR. A privacy flag doesn’t help when the attacker is already inside.
Once the attacker had our credentials, they followed a predictable path. First, they tested each key to see if it was live, in some cases within a few hours of our request. Next, they went looking for data, listing S3 buckets and searching Secrets Manager and Parameter Store. Finally, they tried enabling hosted models in AWS Bedrock, creating Bedrock API keys, and adding their own IAM users to keep access. In total, our bait keys were used for tens of thousands of AWS API calls.
Figure 1. One stolen AWS key. We seeded it on October 21, it was first used the next day, and it was still in use three weeks later. Names are pseudonyms and addresses are partly masked.
One adversary appears to have compromised two of these providers at the same time. Secrets that we sent to those two providers were used together, almost immediately, from a single rented server. The attacker’s tooling changed over the three weeks that we watched it – it started with a script pretending to be Chrome on Windows, moved to Boto3 running inside OrbStack, and later switched to the AWS CLI on Kali Linux.
On November 1, the same attacker added ARTEX, an LLM-driven penetration testing framework. In our case, ARTEX did very little – it made a handful of calls to confirm that keys the attacker already held were still live. Two of the user agents it sent, artex-agent and artex-judge-r121, don’t appear in any public indicator list that we could find, and are worth adding to your detections.
Figure 2. Tools that were used against our planted secrets, including the AI pentest agent (first row) and the Kali environment (aws-cli row).
The good news for defenders is that these tools tend to announce themselves – both Kali and ARTEX showed up in the user agent that AWS records in CloudTrail. The bad news is that this self-identification was only present in a small fraction of traffic.

Figure 4. One day of incidents, grouped by source network. The same scraper networks show up against prompts that we sent through several different routers.
The strangest incident involved a company chat. Twenty-four days after we sent a prompt to one Chinese provider, the secret link from that prompt was posted into a Feishu chat channel (Feishu is known as Lark outside of China). Toyota, Nissan, and Honda to standardize parts to lower costs
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-22
04:04:56 UTC
13:04:56 KST

- Toyota, Nissan, and Honda will begin standardizing some vehicle parts in an effort to lower production costs, according to a report by Automotive News.
- The automakers will start with equalized quality criteria for steel, consistent protocols for plastics, and a universal color-coding scheme for wiring harnesses.
- Discussions are starting with Japan’s domestic suppliers, though international suppliers will be looped in soon.
Japan’s three largest automakers have agreed to begin standardizing vehicle parts in an effort to reduce costs, according to Automotive News. The outlet reports that Japan Automobile Manufacturers Association chairman Koji Sato, who is also the vice chairman of Toyota, announced the decision at a news conference in Tokyo this week.
In the short term, the standardization efforts will begin with unified quality criteria for steel, consistent protocols for plastics and resins, and a universal color-coding scheme for wiring harnesses. In the long term, the Japan Automobile Manufacturers Association has plans to move to more components that cover the entire car. The three areas were chosen as the first to be standardized because they are parts that are generally not noticed by consumers, and they shouldn’t have an impact on any brand’s individual identity. All three are also areas that account for significant production costs for the automakers, meaning they can have an immediate impact.
For Sale Near You
See all results for used Toyota for sale near 97347
The first discussions of standardization are set to include Japan’s domestic automotive parts suppliers, though international suppliers will be brought into the fold in time. The first standardization efforts are set to go into place next year, according to the AN report.
The push to simplify and lower costs comes as Japan’s seven automakers, including Toyota, Nissan, Honda, Mazda, Subaru, Mitsubishi, and Suzuki, work to fend off the increasingly competitive list of Chinese automakers. The automakers are said to hope that they can channel the money saved by homogenizing into new innovations.
“We all share this strong sense of crisis, creating a momentum for change,” Sato said. “Based upon our sense of a crisis, we decided to choose these three areas.”

Together.ai: Prompt Leakage for Requests
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-22
03:07:27 UTC
12:07:27 KST

We are notifying customers about a security incident involving a subset of prompts submitted to Together AI’s serverless inference service in June and July 2026. An unauthorized party downloaded stored prompt samples after a credential for a private repository was exposed in a public code repository.
We apologize for this incident. We are reviewing the affected data to determine what information was exposed and which customers require direct notification.
What happened
As part of routine software testing, we collected prompt samples authorized for that use and stored them in a private Hugging Face repository. The samples came from requests to the MiniMax M3, GLM-5.2, and Kimi K2.6 models, primarily on June 24, July 4, July 8, and July 14, 2026.
During development of our public GitHub repository, xorl, an AI coding agent committed a credential that provided access to the private Hugging Face repository. Our automated scanning detected the exposed credential, and we reverted the commit.
During the exposure window, an unauthorized party obtained the credential and used it to download the stored prompt samples. The party subsequently searched those samples for credentials to other services and tested whether those credentials were valid. Five of the credentials tested were canary credentials associated with a Dragos security experiment.
Investigation and scope
We have worked with an external forensic investigation firm and law enforcement to investigate the incident.
Based on our review, we have not identified evidence of additional prompt leakage beyond this incident. Our review of the affected prompt contents is ongoing to determine whether sensitive personal information or other customer data was exposed. We will contact affected customers directly as needed with the findings relevant to them.
Changes we have made
Following the incident, we strengthened controls around development workflows, credentials, and customer data. These changes include:
- Using scoped or short-lived credentials, and keyless access where supported, to reduce the potential impact of credential exposure.
- Checking all pull requests for credentials.
- Making zero data retention (ZDR) the default setting for new accounts.
- Updating our development processes governing the use of AI coding agents.
What customers can do
Customers handling sensitive prompt data should enable ZDR in their account settings.
If you have questions about this incident or its potential impact on your organization, contact security@together.ai.
Vipul Ved Prakash
CEO and Co-founder, Together AI

The Most Popular Car Colors in America
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-22
02:47:18 UTC
11:47:18 KST

Highlights:
- White is the most popular car color, with 25.7% market share, followed closely by black and gray
- Gray made the biggest gain in popularity over the past 30 years, rising from 3.6% market share in 1996 to 22.9% in 2025
- Grayscale colors (white, black, gray, and silver) now make up 80.4% of the car market, up from 47.3% in 1996
- Red and green now appear on fewer than one in 10 vehicles, with their combined market share falling from 33.5% in 1996 to 9.2% in 2025
- Grayscale colors are most popular in the truck category, with 83.5% market share
- The most colorful category is sports cars, with 36.2% market share for non-grayscale colors
White was the most popular car color 30 years ago and it maintains that ranking today. When combined with the prominence of black (23.4%) and gray (22.9%), the top three most popular colors hold 72% of market share for modern car colors. It’s worth noting that silver, the fourth grayscale color, has gained just over 1 percentage point in popularity over the past 30 years, moving from 7.3% to 8.4% market share since 1996. Silver now trails blue, which holds 9.1% market share.
iSeeCars analyzed the colors of over 22 million used cars from model years 1996 - 2025 sold from January 2025 to May 2026 to determine the market share for each color.
Grayscale colors dominate today’s market
Grayscale colors now account for 80.4% of the car market, while once-prominent colors such as red and green have lost a substantial amount of market share. After nearly three decades of steady growth, grayscale market share has remained close to 80% since the 2020 model year, suggesting the long-running prominence of grayscale cars may have reached its limit.“Up until a few years ago it looked like non-grayscale cars would completely vanish,” said iSeeCars Executive Analyst Karl Brauer. “But the market share of grayscale cars finally stabilized in 2020, at around 80%, suggesting we’ll see at least a sliver of creative car colors going forward.”
Annual trends for individual colors
The movement toward grayscale developed in stages, rather than through the steady rise of a single color. Silver was an early beneficiary, increasing from 7.3% market share for the 1996 model year to roughly 19% in the mid-2000s before declining to 8.4% by the 2025 model year.
Gray followed a different path, increasing in market share consistently through the entire period, ultimately rising from only 3.6% to 22.9% and nearly matching black.
Black and white, which were always popular colors, also gained substantial market share, with black’s popularity growing mostly in the early 2000s’ model years and white gaining share starting in the 2010 model year. Conversely, red and green, which ranked as the 2nd and 4th most popular colors in the 1996 model year, declined substantially throughout much of the 30-year period, with each color’s market share now in single digits. Most other colors either lost what little market share they had or maintained their relatively low popularity. Orange was the only non-grayscale color to increase in popularity, though it remains rare at just 0.3% market share.
“The rise of grayscale cars had to come from somewhere, and it was primarily the fall of red and green, though nearly every non-grayscale shade has lost ground since 1996,” said Brauer.
Trucks: Grayscale colors nearly doubled
Trucks have undergone the largest move toward grayscale colors of any vehicle category. Black, gray, silver, and white increased from 43.4% of 1996 trucks to 83.5% of the 2025 model year, almost doubling in market share. White is the clear leader at 33.6%. This is a higher share for white than the overall market, and it means more than one in three trucks now has a white finish. Red, blue, and green, which collectively represented nearly half of 1996 trucks, account for less than 15% of late-model trucks.
“Trucks are seeing much more than a touch of gray, with the segment going from less than 1 gray truck in 20 in 1996 to 1 in 5 today,” said Brauer.
SUVs: Green and red lose the most ground
SUVs have also shifted from being relatively colorful to being dominated by white, black, gray, and silver. Grayscale colors rose from 43.8% of SUVs in 1996 to 79.3% in 2025, an increase of 80.9%. Green, which ranked second on 1996 SUVs with a 21.3% share, now represents only 2.9% of 2025 SUVs. Red also fell sharply, while blue remained comparatively stable and increased slightly to 9.5%.
“SUV market share has grown more than any other vehicle segment since 1996, but SUV color diversity went the opposite direction during that timeframe,” said Brauer.
Passenger cars: Gray grows, silver declines
Passenger cars have embraced gray more strongly than any other vehicle category. Gray increased from just 3.8% of passenger cars from the 1996 model year to 28.4% for 2025, a relative increase of 652.1%. It now leads both black and white. Silver moved in the opposite direction, declining from 11.2% to 7.4%, while red and green also lost substantial share.
“Gray has gained ground in every vehicle segment, and it’s the most dominant color for today’s passenger cars,” said Brauer.
Sports cars: The most colorful category
Sports cars remain the clearest exception to the increasingly grayscale vehicle market. Non-grayscale colors account for 36.2% of sports cars, nearly twice their 19.6% share across the overall market. Blue, yellow, purple, and orange all gained share. Red declined substantially but remains more common among sports cars than in any other category, appearing on 10.8% of the segment.
“Relative to every other vehicle segment, sports cars still offer a veritable rainbow of color diversity, illustrating the expressive nature of these vehicles and their owners,” said Brauer.
Will we ever go back to a colorful car culture?
The vehicle market has changed from one with a broad mix of colors to one overwhelmingly concentrated around white, black, silver, and gray. While the combined grayscale share has stopped rising as quickly as it once did, colorful vehicles remain far less common than they were 30 years ago. Sports cars provide the strongest evidence that color still has a place in the market, while trucks, SUVs, and passenger cars continue to favor neutral finishes. “Does the recent grayscale plateau signal the beginning of a return to more expressive colors across all segments?” asked Brauer. “That will depend on the paint choices automakers offer and the car colors consumers demand in the years ahead.”
Methodology
iSeeCars analyzed the colors of over 22 million used cars from model years 1996 - 2025 sold from January 2025 to May 2026. The share of each color within each model year was calculated, as was the difference in share between model years 1996 and 2025.
About iSeeCars
iSeeCars helps millions of people make smarter car decisions and find the best deals by leveraging data-driven research and unbiased analysis. iSeeCars Research conducts independent studies that provide key insights on vehicles, market trends, and the automotive industry, including Best Cars Rankings and the annual Longest Lasting Cars Study. With the goal of making car shopping easier and more transparent, iSeeCars.com analyzes millions of car listings and offers valuable resources like iVIN Reports, Window Stickers, and Build Sheets. iSeeCars has saved users over $452 million using over 25 billion data points and proprietary algorithms to objectively analyze, score, and rank new and used vehicles.

Several Waymo Self-Driving Cars Stalled in Traffic as San Francisco's Streets Flooded
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-22
02:34:00 UTC
11:34:00 KST

Several Waymo Self-Driving Cars Stalled in Traffic as San Francisco's Streets Flooded (sfist.com) 35
			
		 	
				San Francisco's streets flooded this week after heavy rainfall — confusing several Waymo self-driving cars. The San Francisco blog Sfist describes what happened after the flooding in some places rose as high as three feet:
Two lanes of Oak Street by the Golden Gate Park Panhandle were flooded Wednesday morning, with water spouting out of manhole covers, leading to a traffic backup that was made worse by a Waymo that couldn't figure its way out of the situation. The Waymo vehicle came to a stop in front of the spouting water, blocking one of the only passable lanes and worsening the traffic situation, as seen in the video below that is going viral...  ["Other drivers had to veer into deeper water to weave around it," reports the San Francisco Chronicle.]
Elsewhere in the city, Waymo rider Edward Frazer posted the video below to X, also around 10:20 am, saying that he had been in a Waymo for an hour and a half trying to get from North Beach to Mission Bay, with the vehicle pulling over around 10 times as it encountered flooded streets. The video shows the Waymo on what appears to be Townsend Street near Adobe's headquarters, stalled as water puddles in front of it. 
Waymo subsequently suspended all service for the rest of the day. 
But "frozen robotaxis were already clogging San Francisco streets by the time Waymo decided to rein in the fleet," reports the San Francisco Chronicle:
 Rider Amy Gutierrez said her Waymo suddenly stopped on Fell Street at the intersection of Masonic Avenue, where rain was coursing into rivers. She contacted company support and was transferred to three specialists who tried to move the jammed vehicle.   It took a half-hour of false starts before they managed to remotely maneuver it to the right lane.  "We were kind of just stuck there — cars were honking," she said, and other drivers were frustrated as traffic backed up. "One person drove by smacking their window, like 'Get out of the way.'" 
Or, as the New York Post puts it, "Waymos spark terror as they trap drivers in rapidly rising floodwater as monster storm hits California city."
Waymos have sparked chaos in San Francisco as they trapped passengers in cars despite rapidly rising floods.  Multiple self-driving vehicles were paralyzed across the streets and left those inside frantically calling the company to help get them to safety.
One was trapped in the middle of a busy intersection when the car refused to go around an overflowing drain, leaving other drivers to go on the wrong side of the road to get around.... 
The San Francisco Chronicle also remembers that last year's power blackout caused 1,600 Waymo vehicles to stall "because they required a 'confirmation check' from a human operator before navigating stoplights after the power was cut. Hundreds of requests overwhelmed the system, leaving San Francisco streets full of paralyzed self-driving vehicles."
Thanks to Slashdot reader AzWa Snowbird for sharing the news.

OpenRouter off-boards 3 providers, pulls 125 endpoints from zero data retention
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-22
02:14:14 UTC
11:14:14 KST

Data Processing Incidents on Inference Providers
OpenRouter ·
Earlier this week, security researchers reported that certain inference providers had data compromised or otherwise shown evidence that their data policies were not being upheld. We have been collaborating closely with the researchers and with providers to understand the findings and encourage the providers to address them.
These findings did not show any breach or unauthorized access of OpenRouter. The results of this testing revealed new findings about specific inference providers. A live list of the security announcements we have received from providers is below.
As the operating layer between customers and a fast-moving ecosystem of models and providers, we take data policy enforcement very seriously. We have taken the following actions:
- Off-boarded three providers from OpenRouter, based on the results of our investigations.
- Removed 125 endpoints from Zero Data Retention (ZDR) eligibility. The removals included providers flagged by researchers, plus additional endpoints that we removed as a precautionary measure.
- Notified all potentially affected customers.
The following work is underway:
- We are adapting the researchers’ testing approach so we can run these checks on an ongoing basis.
- We are conducting technical verification of network paths to identify instances where requests and responses may be being forwarded to third parties.
- We are requiring appropriate assurances and validation before restoring ZDR eligibility to any endpoint removed in this review.
We are grateful for the Dragos report. As we integrate similar testing techniques into OpenRouter, we will keep raising the bar, and providers that want to be on OpenRouter will need to meet it.
Provider security announcements
- Novita: Privacy notice regarding MiniMax requests on Novita
- Phala: November preliminary incident report
- Together: Security event: prompt leakage for requests in June and July of 2026
Not all affected providers have posted publicly. We have notified affected users regardless of whether there is a public post. We are encouraging all providers to create incident write-ups and will keep this list updated.
